The Nature of Phonological Differences in Dyslexia and Stuttering: Evidence from Phonemic, Semantic, and Design Fluency Tasks

To fix (6)

To fix Power Analysis Check

What we found

We detected 1 potential power analysis.

Show details

We used the LLM model ‘vllm/DeepSeek-V41-Flash-1017-FP8’ to check the contents of 3 paragraphs that contained words suggesting they might contain power analyses.

Some essential information could not be detected: statistical_test, sample_size, software

power_type statistical_test sample_size alpha_level power effect_size effect_size_metric software
apriori NA NA 0.05 0.95 2.22 Cohen’s d NA
text
Based on the prior literature and the typical effect sizes reported in verbal fluency research involving neurodivergent participants (e.g., Lopez-Zamora et al., 2025), an effect size of d = 2.22 was decided upon for the main effect of the participant group on phonemic fluency. This choice of effect size strikes a reasonable balance between detecting subtle but clinically relevant differences and ensuring feasibility given resource constraints. With an alpha level set at α = 0.05 (two-tailed) and a desired power of 0.95 (95%), our power analysis indicated that a sample size of approximately seven per group was required to detect this effect size.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 2 imprecise p values out of 70 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p < .05 To maintain narrative clarity and focus on core theoretical questions, the text below highlights primary high-level findings and statistically significant predictors (p < .05) from the hierarchical regression models.
p < .05 Measures included Author Recognition Test (ART); Title Recognition Test (TRT), British Picture Vocabular y Scale (BPVS), Test of Word 648 Reading Efficiency Sight Word Reading Efficiency (TOWRE_S), Test of Word Reading Efficiency Phoneme Decoding (TOWRE_P); Test of Irregular Word 649 Reading Efficiency (TIWRE); Gray Silent Reading Test (GSRT); a Degrees of freedom ( df) are recalculated as homogeneity of variance was unequal (Fligner-650 Killeen test, p < .05).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (3)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 33 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

p = .73 For Phonological Precision, the neurotypical adults scored significantly higher than both the self-reported AWS (t(122) = 6.81, p < .001) and the self-reported dyslexic adults (t(122) = 7.38, p < .001), with no significant difference being found between the self-reported AWS and the self-reported dyslexic adults (t(122) = -0.35, p = .73).

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 2 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Spreadsheet Formatting

We examined 1 spreadsheet file in the repository; 1 has at least one formatting issue.

File Sheet Issue Detail
Verbal_fluency_codebook.xlsx Sheet1 Merged cells 2 merged ranges (A76:XFD76, A34:XFD34) break the rectangular grid.

Spreadsheet formatting such as colour, merged cells, and blank rows/columns is not preserved when data are read programmatically or exported to CSV. Store data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable, no colour-encoded meaning) so it is machine-readable.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 3 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

The participants were recruited via the participant recruitment platform Prolific, except for the self-reported AWS who were recruited via Action for Stammering in Children and Stamma.

Based on this approach, we recruited a total of 212 participants (77 neurotypical adults, 68 self-reported dyslexic adults and 67 self-reported AWS) but we removed 11 neurotypical adults due to their not completing the study, stating they used external devices or not following the instructions, while six self-reported dyslexic adults and five self-reported AWS were removed due to failing the manipulation check or using external devices (see the Procedure for details.

Initially, the participants who had been recruited via Prolific were invited to complete demographic information concerning whether they had received a formal diagnosis of dyslexia or diagnosis of any other neurodivergent profiles.

Neurotypical adults were invited to continue Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering if they answered “no” to the Prolific screening question “Literacy difficulties”.

If the participants answered “yes” to the Prolific screening question “Literacy difficulties”, they were identified as self-reported dyslexic adults.

All the participants were asked to complete two brief screening questionnaires assessing stuttering and dyslexia to support this initial allocation to the different participant groups.

To be included in the self-reported AWS group, the participants were required to have a selfidentified history of developmental stuttering with onset during childhood.

The participants completed explicit compliance and task-integrity instructions prior to beginning the experimental battery.

Before starting the study, the participants were presented with mandatory guidelines emphasizing the scientific necessity of unassisted, natural language processing.

Specifically, at the end of the experiment, the participants completed self-report questions assessing whether they used memory strategies or sought external assistance (e.g., pen and paper, online tools, spell check, or another person).

To encourage honest reporting, the participants were explicitly informed prior to completing these questions that their responses would have no impact on their monetary compensation.

To maintain experimental control in the remote environment, the participants completed a mandatory headphone, bot check, and audio check prior to testing, and tasks were enforced in full-screen browser mode to minimize external distractions.

Prolific and Gorilla allowed us to automatically monitor session completion times and network connectivity to detect and filter out extreme speeders.

The data collection process was overseen by the lead author, who monitored task duration metrics and provided direct participant support via Prolific and email messaging throughout the testing period.

The participants were instructed to think about stuttering severity with an emphasis on overt, or observable, features of stuttering.

Before beginning the experiment, all the participants provided informed consent and completed demographic measures including age, gender, and handedness.

To maintain consistency and control for order effects, all the participants completed the assessments in a fixed, identical order.

The second Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering task, Empty Dots Only, followed the identical rules, but the boxes contained both empty and filled dots, and the participants were instructed to use only the empty dots to form the designs.

The final task, Switching, was based on the same principles, but the participants were asked to alternate between using filled and empty dots within each design.

Given the participant pool size (N = 186), it was necessary to reduce the initial 15 variables to just eight to ensure the reliability of the subsequent Principal Component Analysis (PCA), as it typically requires five to 10 participants per variable (Kass & Tinsley, 1979).

By leveraging remote administration via Prolific and online communication, our study recruited a moderate-powered sample (N = 62 per group) that significantly exceeded those reported in previous work (e.g., Gkalitsiou et al., 2018, N = 32 AWS; Smith-Spark et al., 2017, N = 28 dyslexia).

Ethics approval statement:

The study received approval from the first author’s University’s ethics committee (2990, me237).

Following institutional ethics protocols, raw data were destroyed upon study completion, and only aggregated data were retained.

Preregistration Check

We found 1 preregistration.

id title template
yrdex Verbal fluency in dyslexia and stuttering OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
yrdex Our target sample size is 62 neurotypical adults, 62 dyslexic adults and 62 adults who stutter.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Verbal fluency in dyslexia and stuttering
id yrdex
link https://osf.io/yrdex
date_created 2025-07-05T15:43:06.032137
date_modified 2025-06-19T10:11:37.230154
date_registered 2025-07-05T15:43:06.012888
embargo_end_date 2025-08-30T00:00:00Z
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-yrdex-v1
research_questions 1) we predict that dyslexic adults will show lower phonemic fluency, but not semantic or design fluency than neurotypical adults.

2) we predict that adults who stutter will show lower phonemic fluency, but not semantic or design fluency than neurotypical adults.

3) At the fine-grained level, dyslexia and stutter diagnosis will predict a smaller number of switches between subcategories on phonemic fluency, while dyslexia and stutter diagnosis will not predict the size of phonemically related clusters of items.

4) We have no clear prediction for whether there will be group differences for dyslexic people and people who stutter in phonemic fluency, switching or clustering.
study_type Experiment - A researcher randomly assigns treatments to study subjects, this includes field or lab experiments. This is also known as an intervention experiment and includes randomized controlled trials.
blinding No blinding is involved in this study.
study_design_overview The verbal fluency experiment has a 3 (participant group) within-subject design and a hierarchical design. Participant groups are neurotypical readers, people who stutter or dyslexic readers. In addition, we will use separate hierarchical multiple regression analyses to determine whether the presence of dyslexia or stuttering was a significant predictor of total responses generated, valid responses, number of errors, number of switches, words per cluster and the total number of items generated in each quartile of Letter and Category Fluency. The data for Design Fluency will be analysed in terms of the total number of responses generated, the total valid responses, and the total number of errors.

We will additionally collect individual difference measures relevant to dyslexia. These are:
phoneme elision, nonword repetition, memory for digits, rapid letter naming and phoneme reversal, spelling, the Gray silent reading task, test of word reading efficiency sight word and phoneme decoding, test of irregular word reading efficiency, author recognition test, Raven’s matrices, title recognition test and British Picture vocabulary.
randomization There will be no randomization as the individual differences tests will be conducted in the same order.
existing_data Registration prior to creation of data
data_collection_procedures Participants will be recruited using the Gorilla and Prolific interface. They will also receive reimbursement at a rate equivalent to £11.50 per hour. Non-dyslexic readers must be over 18 years old, with native English as their only proficient language, must not have dyslexia, and have normal/corrected vision. Dyslexic readers will follow the same criteria but must include a formal or a self-diagnosis of dyslexia.
sample_size Our target sample size is 62 neurotypical adults, 62 dyslexic adults and 62 adults who stutter.
sample_size_rationale Our study aims to investigate differences in phonemic fluency across three distinct groups: dyslexic adults, adults who stutter, and neurotypical adults. As an individual differences study, our primary goal is to compare performance on phonemic fluency tasks between these groups, specifically examining whether dyslexia and stuttering lead to lower scores compared to neurotypical controls, and to what extent these two neurodivergent conditions differ from each other.

To ensure sufficient statistical power to detect meaningful differences, we have opted for a sample size of 62 participants per group, resulting in a total sample size of 186 adults. This decision is informed by an a priori power analysis conducted in an independent t-test.

Based on prior literature and typical effect sizes observed in verbal fluency research involving clinical and neurodevelopmental populations (Lopez- Zamora et al., 2025), the authors had an effect size of d = 2.22 for the main effect of group on phonemic fluency. This choice of effect size is a reasonable balance between detecting subtle but clinically relevant differences and ensuring feasibility given resource constraints.

With an alpha level of α=0.05 (two-tailed) and a desired power of 0.95 (95%), our power analysis indicates that a sample size of approximately 7 per group is required to detect this effect size. However, as previous studies are likely to have overestimated effect sizes, we multiplied it by 2.5 (Simonsohn, 2015) and as we are planning to get individual differences, we aimed to get approximately 62 participants per group is required. This ensures an 95% chance of correctly rejecting the null hypothesis (i.e., detecting a significant difference if one truly exists) and minimizing the risk of a Type II error (false negative).

While time, money, and personnel are always practical considerations in research, our chosen sample size is primarily driven by the need for adequate statistical power to address our research questions effectively. A 2-hour study duration per participant is deemed sufficient to administer the full battery of verbal fluency tasks and other individual difference measures while minimizing participant fatigue.

In summary, the sample size of 62 participants per group is justified by an a priori power analysis aiming to detect a medium effect size in phonemic fluency, ensuring sufficient statistical power to draw meaningful conclusions about the differences between dyslexic, stuttering, and neurotypical adults.
stopping_rule 62 participants per participant group. If participants need to be excluded (e.g. because they fail to complete a task, see Data Exclusion section), they will be replaced to ensure that the final data set has 62 participants per participant group.
manipulated_variables Phonemic fluency - name or write as many words beginning with a specific phoneme as fast as possible in 60 seconds
Semantic fluency - name or write as many words from a specific category as fast as possible in 60 seconds
Design fluency - complete as many designs as fast as possible in 60 seconds
The individual difference measures:
Author recognition test- select as many correct authors as possible and not select the foils to avoid penalty
Title recognition test- select as many correct book titles as possible and not select the foils to avoid penalty
Spelling- you are presented a word, spell them as correctly as possible
British Picture Vocabulary- select the correct word representative of the audio
Gray Silent Reading Test- respond correctly to the comprehension question based on the text
Test of Word Reading Efficiency: Sight word reading- read as many words as possible within 45 seconds.
Test of Word Reading Efficiency: phoneme decoding-read as many pseudowords as possible within 45 seconds.
Test of irregular word reading efficiency: read irregular words
Rapid Letter Naming: name as many letters as possible and time taken (s) is the measure
Nonword repetition-repeat nonwords aloud
Memory for digits- repeat memory aloud
Phoneme elision- hear a word and remove a phoneme (e.g. remove /p/ from spider to make spy).
Phoneme reversal- hear a nonword and reverse it to make a word (tis becomes sit).
Raven Standard Matrices - select a panel that matches the overarching pattern.
measured_variables Separate hierarchical multiple regression analyses were used to determine whether
the presence of dyslexia or stuttering was a significant predictor of total responses generated, valid responses, number of errors, number of switches, words per cluster and the total number of items generated in each quartile of Phonemic and Category Fluency.

For the individual differences measures:
Author recognition test- accuracy
Title recognition test- accuracy
Spelling- correct spelling
British Picture Vocabulary-accuracy
Gray Silent Reading Test- accuracy
Test of Word Reading Efficiency: number of words correctly read within 45 seconds
Test of Word Reading Efficiency: number of pseudowords correctly read within 45 seconds
Test of irregular word reading efficiency: accuracy
Rapid Letter Naming: speed to name all letters
Nonword repetition-correct pronunciation
Memory for digits- accuracy
Phoneme elision- accuracy
Phoneme reversal- accuracy
Raven -accuracy
statistical_tests A general linear mixed model (GLMM) will be used to analyse reaction times and accuracy, using the lme4 package in R. Significance is set at α = 0.05 and if the t values are above 2, the p value is significant (Baayen et al., 2008). The GLMM is conducted on dependent measures, as linear regression coefficients are directly interpretable when interactions or fixed effects are included, making GLMM safer to use. In addition, a linear model will be used as opposed to using a general logistic mixed model since the former produces unbiased estimates of effects on binary outcomes (Gomila, 2020). In the event that we fail to replicate the standard transposed word effect in our initial analysis of the accuracy data, we will re-analyse this data using the type of model that has been used in prior investigations of this phenomenon (i.e. logistic) in order to ensure that the difference between our work and prior studies is not simply due to the use of different statistical approaches.
Statistical analyses will be performed only on the data concerning the ungrammatical sequences, thus the fixed effects are effects of group-sum coded (-1 dyslexia and 1 neurotypical) or (-1 stuttering and 1 neurotypical) and (-1 stuttering and 1 dyslexia). The contrasts conducted on each fixed effect will be sum-coded to make coefficients directly interpretable. In addition, the random effects will include an item and subject intercept with random slopes for condition being included in the random model (see below): accuracy ~ 1 + Component1 * group +(1 |Subject) + (1 group|item).
In all cases, the maximal random structure model will include the interaction of both conditions with both subjects and items (Barr et al., 2013). A fully random model is used whenever possible.
However, fully specified models often fail to converge. If the model fails to converge, we will simplify the random slopes, removing interactions before main effects in the order of least variance explained until the model converges (Veldre & Andrews, 2014).

The individual differences will be transformed into a composite score if they are highly correlated and placed in a PCA.
transformations The individual differences will be z-transformed to make the measures more likely matched.
inference_criteria We will use the standard p <.05 criteria, using t values must be above 2. In addition, to further assess whether the non-significant effect indeed supports the null hypothesis, we will calculate the Bayes factor, using ‘BayesFactor’ package in R (see Rouder et al., 2012 for details). This result will provide evidence in favour of the null hypothesis. The BF must be below 1/3 to argue in favour of the null hypothesis.
data_exclusion_criteria If a participant fails to complete the experiment, they will not be included in the analysis. If participants scores at ceiling in memory for digits and they state that they were cheating, their data will be removed from the data set prior to analysis.
missing_data_handling Data partially missing because a participant quit study halfway through will be excluded; if applicable, data partially missing due to unforeseen software errors will be included in the main categorical analyses. However, to ensure we obtain additional observations due to unforeseen software errors, we will use multiple imputation (Woods et al., 2021). In addition, we will investigate any item responses resulting from missing data not at random. For instance, if only 10% of participants respond incorrectly to some items, while a few other items are responded to incorrectly by around 60% of participants, then we will discuss why this may have happened which may lead to additional exploratory analyses (Woods et al., 2021).
additional_comments Elsherif, M. M., Wheeldon, L. R., & Frisson, S. (2021). Do dyslexia and stuttering share a processing deficit?. Journal of Fluency Disorders, 67, 105827.
