How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Objective: Dyslexia and stuttering are both defined by phonological processing challenges. Using executive fluency tasks, the current study investigated whether these challenges reflect phonological representations themselves or access to intact representations during selfreported adults who stutter (AWS) and self-reported dyslexic adults.

Method: An online, unsupervised sample of 62 self-reported AWS, 62 self-reported dyslexic adults and 62 neurotypical adults completed a modified, typed version of semantic and phonemic fluency, as well as a mouse-drawn version of design fluency. We analyzed switching (shifting between subcategories; proxy for retrieval efficiency) and cluster size (words per subcategory, proxy for representational integrity).

Results: Controlling for nonverbal IQ and language measures, hierarchical regression showed that dyslexia and stuttering significantly predicted reduced overall phonemic output, whereas semantic and design fluency remained unaffected. Both groups showed reduced switching, though more pronounced in the dyslexia group. Conversely, only stuttering predicted reduced cluster size.

Conclusion: Given the heterogeneity of these neurodivergent profiles, these findings suggest that, in dyslexia, access to phonological representations is affected. Conversely, stuttering affects the representations themselves. For individuals presenting these specific cognitive signatures, interventions could be tailored toward either retrieval strategies or representational strengthening.

Introduction

Developmental dyslexia and developmental stuttering are neurodivergent profiles that have both been linked to challenges with phonological (i.e., speech sound) processing (Elsherif et al., 2021). While these neurodivergent groups are often studied in isolation, a direct comparison of their cognitive performance could provide important theoretical insights into the commonalities and differences between them. Further to this, both neurodivergent profiles tend to be studied predominantly in childhood but are experienced over the lifespan. While research informing early diagnosis and intervention is clearly very important, there is considerably less understanding of how these profiles manifest themselves in cognition beyond childhood and adolescence and, thus, how adults with these profiles might be best supported. In tandem with addressing important theoretical issues, samples from adult Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering populations were therefore recruited to the current study as a contribution towards redressing this age-related imbalance in knowledge. A critical, yet unresolved, question is the extent to which the phonological challenges of both dyslexia and stuttering are compounded or mediated by executive function, a set of higher-order cognitive abilities that includes inhibition, set-shifting, and updating (e.g., Diamond, 2013; Fisk & Sharp, 2004; Miyake et al., 2000).

While previous research has documented executive function differences in both dyslexia (e.g., Brosnan et al., 2002; Smith-Spark et al., 2016; Varvara et al., 2014) and stuttering (Ntourou et al., 2018; Ofoe et al., 2018 Ofoe et al., , 2026;; Treleaven & Coalson, 2020), the relationship between these executive processes and phonological performance remains debated. The research reported in the current paper, therefore, investigated verbal and nonverbal executive fluency in self-reported dyslexic adults, self-reported adults who stutter (AWS) 1 , and neurotypical adults. The overall aim of this study was to evaluate three competing theoretical accounts by examining how domain-general executive functioning interacts with both the quality of phonological representations and the mechanisms of phonological access. By contrasting these dynamics across dyslexia and stuttering, we sought to clarify whether observed difficulties are due to storage, retrieval, or a domain-general executive constraint.

Neurocognitive Profiles: Dyslexia, Stuttering, and Executive Function Developmental Dyslexia is a neurodivergent profile manifesting in approximately seven to 10% of the population (e.g., Peterson & Pennington, 2012). It is argued to arise from complex interactions between genetic, neural, cognitive, and environmental factors (rather than from a single difficulty) and is thus best considered within a multifactorial explanatory framework (e.g., 1 Although there is a debate concerning person-first and identity-first language within academia and the neurodivergent community, recent research has shown that, on average, dyslexic adults prefer identity-first language over person-first language and adults who stutter prefer person-first language over identity-first language (Pearson et al., 2025). We have used these preferences to guide our choice of nomenclature. McGrath et al., 2020; Nicolson & Fawcett, 2010; Pennington, 2006; Stein & Walsh, 1997; see review by Huettig et al., 2018). The dyslexic profile frequently involves distinct phonological processing difficulties (e.g., Vellutino et al., 2004) alongside broader executive function differences (e.g., Bednarek et al., 2004; Brosnan et al., 2002; Facoetti & Turatto, 2000; Griffiths, 1991; Mahe et al., 2014; Reiter et al., 2005; Smith-Spark et al., 2016; Smith-Spark & Fisk, 2007; Smith-Spark & Gordon, 2022). These latter challenges are well-documented across the lifespan in both children (for systematic reviews and meta-analyses, see Booth et al., 2010; Brown et al., 2026; Lonergan et al., 2019) and in adults (e.g., Brosnan et al., 2002; Smith-Spark et al., 2016). However, determining what underlies these challenges often introduces a task impurity problem (e.g., Phillips, 1997), since many executive function assessment tasks inherently tax phonological processing abilities to some degree (e.g., Smith-Spark et al., 2017).

Similarly, developmental stuttering is a complex neurodevelopmental condition affecting approximately 8.5% of the paediatric population and 0.81% of adults (Yairi & Ambrose, 2013).

It is characterised by a disruption to the motor planning necessary for speech (Smith & Weber, 2017). Like dyslexia, models conceptualize stuttering within multifactorial frameworks (e.g., Smith & Weber, 2017), wherein speech disruptions emerge from the interplay of neural, speech-motor, linguistic, emotional, and cognitive variables (Bowers et al., 2018). Both children who stutter (CWS) and AWS frequently demonstrate challenges with executive function, particularly when inhibitory control and cognitive flexibility are required (Anderson & Ofoe, 2019; Eggers et al., 2012 Eggers et al., , 2013;; Eichorn et al., 2018; Kakuta & Kawasaki, 2025; Lescht et al., 2022). These executive function differences may create a dynamic feedback loop: managing complex cognitive loads can tax executive resources heavily during speech production, while the ongoing energy expenditure required to navigate a non-fluent speech system can, in turn, deplete those available executive resources (Anderson & Ofoe, 2019; Eggers et al., 2012; Eichorn et al., 2018; Kakuta & Kawasaki, 2025; Lescht et al., 2022). It remains strongly debated whether these cognitive variations reflect generalized cognitive challenges or domain-specific language constraints (Bahrami et al., 2014; Lescht et al., 2022).

Although dyslexia and stuttering present distinct language processing profiles, research suggests the two conditions are not mutually exclusive. Elsherif et al. (2021) identified a significant co-occurrence rate of 30-40%, an overlap which far exceeds what would be expected by chance. By evaluating individual differences across orthographic, phonological, and semantic domains, these authors sought to isolate the source of this co-occurrence. The authors found that the two neurodivergent groups showed similar scores in measures related to phonological processing (see also Elsherif et al., 2025). Because of this comparability, it is difficult to determine whether a shared core difference in both phonological processing and executive function abilities affects the two neurodivergent groups. Within a contemporary multifactorial framework (McGrath et al., 2021; Smith & Weber, 2017), converging profiles raise a critical question as to whether these shared behavioural outcomes stem from a common phonological origin, overlapping executive demands or distinct interacting cognitive mechanisms. Answering this question is critical for refining the diagnostic and the theoretical boundaries between dyslexia and stutter.

Theoretical Accounts of Phonological Challenges in Dyslexia and Stutter

Theoretical accounts of the phonological difficulties in dyslexia are generally divided between two primary perspectives. The manifestation of dyslexia traits is heavily modulated by crosslinguistic factors such as orthographic transparency, which modulates the mapping of phonological representations to written symbols (see Yael et al., 2015; Ziegler & Goswami, 2005). The prevailing degraded representation hypothesis suggests that phonological representations themselves are less robust and distinct in dyslexic individuals (Leong et al., 2011; Ziegler & Goswami, 2005). These degraded representations then disrupt higher-level processing and reading development, particularly in opaque orthographies such as English.

An alternative perspective, the delayed phonological access hypothesis (Ramus & Szenkovits, 2008), posits that phonological representations remain intact but are difficult to retrieve under specific cognitive pressures. This account suggests that the challenges become most apparent during tasks that require the explicit manipulation of speech sounds, speeded Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering retrieval or short-term memory, meaning that dyslexic traits may be exacerbated by high task demands. This explanation has been supported empirically (e.g., Boets et al., 2013; Dickie et al., 2013; Ramus et al., 2013; Szenkovits et al., 2016) and the data reported in the adult dyslexia literature largely align with this account.

Given that many AWS exhibit phonological processing challenges (e.g., Elsherif et al., 2021; Pelczarski & Yaruss, 2014), the theoretical framework of the degraded phonological representation hypothesis (e.g., Leong et al., 2011) and the delayed phonological access hypothesis (e.g., Ramus & Szenkovits, 2008) provide a useful lens for investigating the underlying phonological processing mechanisms in this neurodivergence too. As noted previously, a representational account (Ziegler & Goswami, 2005) posits that the underlying phonological units themselves are structurally unstable or incomplete, while an access-based account suggests that internal phonological representations remain intact but become disrupted during real-time, time-pressured retrieval. The EXPLAN theory (Howell & Au-Yeung, 2002) directly aligns with the latter phonological access framework, proposing that stuttering arises from a temporal processing mismatch between linguistic planning and motor execution.

Under the EXPLAN model, when motor execution outpaces linguistic planning, speakers either stall execution (repeating planned words or pausing) or advance into partially encoded plans (Ning et al., 2017). Evaluating these competing representational and access mechanisms within a transparent multifactorial framework can help to clarify whether the overlapping behavioral profiles found in dyslexia and stuttering reflect shared transdiagnostic mechanisms or distinct cognitive pathways. In the current study, performance on executive fluency tasks was used to directly investigate this issue.

Verbal Fluency as a Measure of Executive Function

Verbal fluency requires the generation of non-repeating items under time limits, typically 60 seconds, and following specific rules (Villalobos et al., 2022 (Villalobos et al., , 2023)). The most frequently administered verbal fluency tasks are phonemic fluency, where participants produce words starting with a specific letter while avoiding proper nouns, repetitions, plurals or names (e.g., Benton, 1968; Borkowski et al., 1967), and semantic fluency (generating items from a specific category) (Newcombe, 1969).

Verbal fluency performance can be measured with coarse-grained markers (e.g., total number of items produced or correct number items generated) and finer-grained indices (e.g., cluster size and number of switches) (e.g., Dorchies et al., 2024; Troyer et al., 1997). Clustering typically reflects the number of closely related items generated in succession. Clustering is driven by automatic spreading of activation between conceptually or phonologically related words (Rosen & Engle, 1997). For example, after producing the word "fat", a participant may list other words such as 'fan', 'far' and 'fad' or apples, a participant may list other fruits that grow on trees such as 'pears', 'peaches' and 'plums'. Such related items are usually produced in spurts or in temporal clusters that have short intervals between items (e.g., Bousfield & Sedgewick, 1944; Henry & Crawford, 2004; Henry et al., 2015; Troyer et al., 1997). These related items are generated until no more words belonging to that subcategory are available, at which point the individual initiates a search for a new subcategory from which to continue producing items. Switching involves cognitive flexibility in transitioning efficiently between distinct subcategories to guide retrieval (Troyer et al., 1997). For example, after listing all the words that they know beginning with "fa", participants may utter "fit", "fund" and "foot" or may decide to switch from arboreal fruits to vine-growing fruits such as "grapes", "tomatoes".

Switching is a cognitively effortful process that requires roles for strategic search, cognitive flexibility and cognitive control (Mayr, 2002; Troyer, 2000; Troyer et al., 1997). Verbal fluency demands an interplay of language abilities and domain-general skills, including attention, memory, processing speed, and inhibitory control, reflecting both unity and diversity in retrieval processes (e.g., Dorchies et al., 2024; Gustavson et al., 2019; Schmidt et al., 2017; Shao et al., 2014).

Verbal fluency in dyslexia and stuttering

Verbal fluency has been well studied in dyslexic populations (see Smith-Spark et al., 2017, for an overview). Broadly speaking, dyslexia-related phonemic fluency differences are well established (e.g., López-Zamora et al., 2025; Mengsidiou & Marshall, 2019; Shareef et al., 2019; Smith-Spark et al., 2017), while the effect of dyslexia on semantic fluency tasks is less clear cut. Some studies indicate group differences in semantic fluency (e.g., López-Zamora et al., 2025; Shareef et al., 2019), while others do not (e.g., Mengsidiou & Marshall, 2019). To our knowledge, the vast majority of these studies have focused on overall output measures and very few studies have reported average cluster size and switching in dyslexic individuals.

One study report that dyslexic adolescents do not differ in the number of clusters, the number of switches or the size of clusters relative to neurotypical adolescents (e.g., Mielnik et al., 2015), while other studies have found that dyslexic adults showed lower scores only for phonemic switching compared with neurotypical adults (Mengsidiou & Marshall, 2019; Smith-Spark et al., 2017).

Research on verbal fluency in AWS is considerably more limited. However, extant work consistently shows phonemic fluency difficulties alongside preserved semantic fluency across spoken and typed modalities (Bahrami et al., 2014; Lescht et al., 2022). Expanding upon this work, Gkalitsiou and Werle (2026) replicated these findings and took the research a step further by analyzing fine-grained measures of verbal fluency. They found that, while AWS and controls showed similar levels of clustering, AWS exhibited fewer phonemic switches. While valuable insights into lexical and executive performance have been obtained from these studies, it is important to note that verbal fluency measures evaluate the constrained lexical retrieval of isolated words rather than the real-time speech-motor execution dynamics that are characteristic of connected speech in which stuttering typically manifests (Howell & Au-Yeung, 2002). Consequently, it remains unclear whether these switching variations reflect a localized phonological phenomenon or a broader executive function challenge. To address this issue, and to isolate executive function from phonological processing, researchers can use nonverbal alternatives to the phonemic and semantic fluency tasks, such as design fluency tasks (Ruff et al., 1987).

Design fluency requires participants to connect printed dots with straight lines to generate novel patterns within a set time limit. Such tasks assess visuospatial executive skills (Mengisidou & Marshall, 2019) without relying on phonological representations and thus provide an index of domain-general executive control (Chen et al., 2025). Both self-reported dyslexic adults and self-reported AWS would be predicted to show similar levels of performance to neurotypical adults in design fluency, since visuospatial executive skills remain distinct from linguistic processing. However, despite its utility in mapping cognitive profiles, research on design fluency in dyslexia remains both sparse and mixed. Some studies report performance differences (e.g., Reiter et al., 2005), while others find no dyslexia-related group differences (e.g., Griffiths, 1991; Mengisidou & Marshall, 2019; Smith-Spark et al., 2017).

There is even less clarity in the case of stuttering with, to the best of our knowledge, no published studies investigating design fluency.

Therefore, the current literature relating to executive fluency in stuttering and dyslexia highlights a central yet unexplored theoretical tension as to whether the challenges in executive fluency reflect poor phonological representations (that limit the pool of available items or an impoverished lexical storage) or delayed phonological access (where intact representations are harder to retrieve under timed conditions or an executive failure to navigate the network efficiently).

To address this important theoretical point, the current study examined whether lower total executive fluency outputs are driven by the size of retrieved clusters or the efficiency of the search process itself, isolating cluster size from switching, to test whether different neurodivergent profiles share a common mechanical failure in lexical navigation. Furthermore, by providing a fine-grained comparison of the performance of self-reported AWS and selfreported dyslexic adults, the current study also investigated whether their phonemic fluency challenges reflect a shared executive-phonological difficulty or distinct underlying cognitive mechanisms. Finally, by using a non-verbal design fluency task, it could be determined whether the challenges in dyslexia and stuttering stem from phonological or executive function challenges.

Guided by the Lexical Quality Hypothesis (LQH; Perfetti, 1992 Perfetti, , 2007 Perfetti, , 2017;; Perfetti & Hart, 2001 , 2002), which posits that lexical retrieval depends on precision (the specificity and detail of individual representations) and redundancy (the strength of binding across orthographic, phonological, and semantic codes), we incorporated individual-difference measures of language and literacy skills (see Elsherif et al., 2022 Elsherif et al., , 2023 Elsherif et al., , 2025) ) and conducted a Principal Component Analysis (PCA) on orthographic, phonological, and semantic measures to derive latent dimensions of language skill (Acheson et al., 2008). Taking this approach allowed us to test predictions based on three different theories, namely phonological access (Ramus & Szenkovits, 2008), the degraded phonological representation hypothesis (Ziegler & Goswami, 2005) and an explanation based on domain-general executive difficulties (e.g., Smith-Spark & Fisk, 2007).

If executive fluency challenges stem directly from imprecise or noisy phonological representations, then it would be predicted that self-reported dyslexic adults and self-reported AWS would produce smaller cluster sizes in phonemic fluency, while switching frequency and performance on semantic and design fluency would remain comparable to controls. This pattern would be expected by the degraded phonological representation account hypothesis (Ziegler & Goswami, 2005).

Instead, if representations are intact but access and language-bound executive regulation (e.g., competitor suppression during retrieval) are inefficient, both neurodivergent groups would show a reduced number of switches across verbal tasks (phonemic and semantic fluency), while showing normal cluster sizes and unimpaired design fluency. This pattern Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering would be expected on the basis of the delayed access to phonological representation theory (Ramus & Szenkovits, 2008).

Finally, under a domain-general executive hypothesis (e.g., Smith-Spark & Fisk, 2007) would transcend processing modalities if lower performance reflects a broad, non-linguistic challenge in executive function and cognitive flexibility difficulties. From this perspective, both neurodivergent profiles would be expected to show lower total output across all three executive fluency tasks.

Method

Participants Power Analysis

Based on the prior literature and the typical effect sizes reported in verbal fluency research involving neurodivergent participants (e.g., Lopez-Zamora et al., 2025), an effect size of d = 2.22 was decided upon for the main effect of the participant group on phonemic fluency. This choice of effect size strikes a reasonable balance between detecting subtle but clinically relevant differences and ensuring feasibility given resource constraints. With an alpha level set at α = 0.05 (two-tailed) and a desired power of 0.95 (95%), our power analysis indicated that a sample size of approximately seven per group was required to detect this effect size.

To reduce M-Error (Gelman & Carlin, 2014), we applied a 2.5x multiplier to previous sample sizes (Simonsohn, 2015) as an initial step. However, to account for the added complexity of measuring standardized measures of spelling, reading fluency, phonology, vocabulary and reading comprehension, we further adjusted our target to 62 participants per group. This ensured that we had a 95% chance of correctly rejecting the null hypothesis and reduced the risk of Type II error. In addition, this sample size was in line with or larger than other published studies examining verbal fluency in either dyslexia or stuttering (Lescht et al., 2022; Smith-Spark et al., 2017).

Recruitment and Final Sample

The participants were recruited via the participant recruitment platform Prolific, except for the self-reported AWS who were recruited via Action for Stammering in Children and Stamma. Individuals were eligible to take part if they were aged between 18 and 36 years and spoke English as their first language. Based on this approach, we recruited a total of 212 participants (77 neurotypical adults, 68 self-reported dyslexic adults and 67 self-reported AWS) but we removed 11 neurotypical adults due to their not completing the study, stating they used external devices or not following the instructions, while six self-reported dyslexic adults and five self-reported AWS were removed due to failing the manipulation check or using external devices (see the Procedure for details. After removing these participants, we had a final validated sample of 186 British-educated native English participants (62 neurotypical adults, aged 19-36 years (age M = 29.5 years, SD = 4.5, 31 women), 62 self-reported dyslexic adults, aged 20-36 years, (age M = 28.4 years, SD = 4.81, 32 women), and 62 self-reported AWS, aged 20-36 years (age M = 28.2 years, SD = 4.31, 31 women).

Demographic Matching

Education level was recorded as their highest completed academic qualification and was categorized numerically as follows: UK A-levels (coded as 1), Bachelor's degree (coded as 2), Master's degree (coded as 3) and PhD (coded as 4). The dyslexia, AWS and neurotypical groups did not differ significantly in age (F(2,183) = 1.33 p = .27), gender (χ 2 (8, 186) = 9.84, p = .27), handedness (χ 2 (4, 186) = 3.60, p = .46), education level (χ 2 (10, 186) = 16.99, p = .07) or bilingualism (F(2, 183) = 1.17, p = .31).

Inclusion and Ethical Approval

Initially, the participants who had been recruited via Prolific were invited to complete demographic information concerning whether they had received a formal diagnosis of dyslexia or diagnosis of any other neurodivergent profiles. Neurotypical adults were invited to continue Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering if they answered "no" to the Prolific screening question "Literacy difficulties". If the participants answered "yes" to the Prolific screening question "Literacy difficulties", they were identified as self-reported dyslexic adults. This approach was taken to ensure that the self-reported dyslexic participants did indeed have a history of reading difficulties and to ensure that their reading challenges were persistent at the time of testing (see Garvin & Krishnan, 2021, who used the same criteria). All the participants were asked to complete two brief screening questionnaires assessing stuttering and dyslexia to support this initial allocation to the different participant groups.

To be included in the self-reported AWS group, the participants were required to have a selfidentified history of developmental stuttering with onset during childhood. Severity was measured using the validated Brief Stuttering Questionnaire (O'Brian et al., 2004; Gerlach et al., 2021). The second questionnaire was the Abbreviated Adult Reading History Questionnaire or ARHQ-Brief (Feng et al., 2021), designed to ascertain whether the participants encountered challenges concerning their reading during childhood. The participants' scores were summed and divided by the maximum score (24) in order to ensure that they would fit the inclusion criteria for the study. Neurotypical adults were deemed eligible to participate if they reported no neurodivergent profiles during screening and also scored < .32 on the ARHQ-Brief. Self-reported dyslexic adults were deemed eligible to participate based on self-reported diagnosis of dyslexia and an ARHQ-Brief score of > .32. The study received approval from the first author's University's ethics committee (2990, me237).

Materials

Web-based administration of cognitive and verbal measures yields data quality and effect sizes comparable to traditional laboratory testing across diverse adult populations (e.g., Germine et al., 2012; Grootswagers, 2020). The individual difference tasks were presented using http://www.gorilla.sc/ an online experiment platform (Anwyl-Irvine et al., 2020 , 2021). Access was restricted to desktop or laptop computers, as the study was conducted with another unrelated task (that is not reported here). Mobile devices and tablets were automatically blocked via Gorilla's system settings.

Furthermore, data quality control procedures adhered to the best practice recommendations for online cognitive research outlined by Rodd (2024). The participants completed explicit compliance and task-integrity instructions prior to beginning the experimental battery. Before starting the study, the participants were presented with mandatory guidelines emphasizing the scientific necessity of unassisted, natural language processing. Specifically, at the end of the experiment, the participants completed self-report questions assessing whether they used memory strategies or sought external assistance (e.g., pen and paper, online tools, spell check, or another person). To encourage honest reporting, the participants were explicitly informed prior to completing these questions that their responses would have no impact on their monetary compensation. Responses to these checks were screened to identify and exclude data from the participants who relied on unauthorized external aids.

To maintain experimental control in the remote environment, the participants completed a mandatory headphone, bot check, and audio check prior to testing, and tasks were enforced in full-screen browser mode to minimize external distractions. Platform-level controls monitored network connectivity and strictly prevented browser tab switching during task completion. Prolific and Gorilla allowed us to automatically monitor session completion times and network connectivity to detect and filter out extreme speeders. Completion times were continuously tracked, and trials or participant sessions with impossibly fast keypress latencies, completing the experiment in one hour for a two hour experiment, severe timing artifacts, including >3SD from the sample median battery completion time, indicating multi-tasking or task desertion, severe network disruptions or connectivity disruptions were identified and excluded prior to analysis. However, external environmental factors during task accuracy performance were not directly controlled. All sessions were administered asynchronously. The data collection process was overseen by the lead author, who monitored task duration metrics and provided direct participant support via Prolific and email messaging throughout the testing period.

Screening measures AHRQ-Brief

The ARHQ-Brief (Feng et al., 2021) is a 6-item self-report questionnaire to assess the reading history and developmental literacy traits of adults. The participants rated each question on a 4-point Likert scale, yielding a composite proportional score ranging from 0 to 1 (with total raw scores summed and proportioned against the maximum possible score of 24). The items evaluate academic history, screening for early literacy challenges, such as difficulty learning letter names or colors and reversing characters and persistent historical struggles with workload and performance in higher-level English courses. Feng et al. (2021) observed that scores on ARHQ-Brief are strongly correlated to composite reading ability score and the broader, extensively validated full-length Adult Reading History Questionnaire (Pennington & Lefly, 2001; Feng et al., 2021). An optimal classification threshold of .32 was established (Feng et al., 2021), demonstrating robust psychometric utility with a sensitivity of 72.4% and a specificity of 81.5% for identifying self-reported dyslexic adults. In the current study, neurotypical adults were deemed eligible if they reported no neurodivergent profiles during screening and scored < .32 on the ARHQ-Brief. Conversely, self-reported dyslexic adults were deemed eligible based on a self-reported formal diagnosis of dyslexia corroborated by an ARHQ-Brief score > .32. AWS were not screened out based on a dyslexia diagnosis. Given that dyslexia and stuttering frequently co-occur (Elsherif et al., 2021), excluding individuals with co-occurring dyslexia would have resulted in an unrepresentatively narrow neurodivergent profile. Furthermore, any observed challenges with reading aloud in this population must be interpreted cautiously, as they can stem from speech motor blocks and the anticipation of stuttering as opposed to underlying dyslexic difficulties.

Stuttering questionnaire

The Brief stuttering questionnaire (O'Brian et al. 2004; Gerlach et al. 2021) was used to assess the participants' history of stuttering and self-rated severity, rather than relying on snapshot objective measures like the Stuttering Severity Instrument-Fourth Edition (SSI-4; Riley & Bakker, 2009), which can be highly context-dependent and provides a limited snapshot of stuttering on a given day and testing setting (Constantino et al., 2016). Stuttering severity was rated on a 1 to 9 scale (1 = no stuttering,9 = extremely severe) across eight different communication situations, such as conversing with family or speaking on the phone. The participants were instructed to think about stuttering severity with an emphasis on overt, or observable, features of stuttering. This self-assessment method is validated against speechlanguage therapists' ratings (see O'Brian et al., 2004). In line with Gerlach et al. (2021) and Phillips and Myers (2025), the varying level of stuttering was averaged into a composite score. Procedure Manipulation checks. Before beginning the experiment, all the participants provided informed consent and completed demographic measures including age, gender, and handedness. To ensure data integrity and participant authenticity, a multi-stage verification protocol was implemented. This was designed to differentiate between the two target cohorts: self-reported AWS and self-reported dyslexic adults, while excluding automated bot responses or "bad faith" participants attempting to access the study's compensation. This verification was conducted in the form of a phenomenological manipulation check requiring the participants to describe the internal mechanisms of their neurodivergent profile. For the self-reported AWS, the check focused on pre-phonatory physical tension and the cognitive decision-making involved in word substitution ("What words or phrases come to mind when you think about blocks?"). For the self-reported dyslexic adults, the verification probe targeted orthographic retrieval. The participants described the internal cognitive strategies used when phonetic-tographemic mapping fails ("What words or phrases come to mind when you try to see the word or hear the sounds and when it becomes blurry?"). Responses were screened for processoriented markers such as sub-vocalizing to bridge phonetic gaps. These indicators served to Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering distinguish authentic lived experience from superficial or 'textbook' knowledge of the neurodivergent profiles.

General Procedures for standardized tests.

The overall study required each participant to attend a single session remotely, which lasted approximately two hours. To maintain consistency and control for order effects, all the participants completed the assessments in a fixed, identical order. The study was conducted without direct participant-examiner supervision; however, the protocol was standardized to minimize variability. The selection of these measures was guided by the Lexical Quality Hypothesis (Perfetti, 2007), which posits that high-quality lexical representations are formed through the integrated precision of orthographic, phonological, and semantic constituents (detailed further in Table 1). These domains included non-verbal intelligence and measures grouped into four main areas. A comprehensive battery of standardized tests was used to assess four core cognitive-linguistic domains: Orthography, Phonology, Reading Fluency, and Semantics. The first area was orthographic processing, which included three measures: spelling dictation, an author recognition test, and a title recognition test (see Elsherif et al., 2022 Elsherif et al., , 2023 Elsherif et al., , 2025, for further details). The second area, phonological processing, involved four tests: phoneme elision, phoneme reversal, nonword repetition, and memory for digits. The third area, reading fluency, was assessed with four tests: regular word reading, irregular word reading, pseudoword reading, and rapid letter naming. Finally, the fourth area, semantic processing, included two tests: receptive vocabulary, and a reading comprehension test. To ensure the accuracy of data collection, the modality of response was tailored to the specific cognitive task. All orthographic, phonological processing and semantic processing tasks were administered via a typing interface, allowing the participants to record their responses directly. Only tasks inherently requiring oral production, specifically reading fluency measures, such as TOWRE, TIWRE and rapid letter naming, were conducted orally. Specific task descriptions, standardized citations, and the theoretical constructs measured by each are summarized in Table 1].
