Aligning with standard spoken fluency paradigms, accuracy depends on target word generation rather than orthographic correctness, typed entries were evaluated on phonological intent. Responses were scored as correct if they constituted phonologically plausible spellings or phonetic approximations of valid target words. Data processing involved initial automatic string matching, followed by a manual review of all non-identical entries by the first author to verify phonological validity against task-specific rules.

Procedures for the executive fluency tasks

The Phonemic Fluency, Semantic Fluency and Design Fluency subscales from the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (D-KEFS; Delis et al., 2001) standardized battery of tests were used to test the executive function of fluency. The participants performed a series of 60second verbal fluency trials. The first task was Phonemic Fluency, where the participants typed as many distinct words as possible beginning with a specified letter (F, A, or S), while strictly excluding numbers, places or proper names. The second task, Semantic Fluency, required generating words belonging to a specific semantic category, the names of animals.

The third task was Category Switching, where the participants alternated quickly between two different semantic categories, fruits and furniture, for 60 seconds. All responses were typed instead of spoken (to accommodate potential articulatory differences for AWS), a choice of response modality supported by Lescht et al. (2022), who have argued that the phonological challenges for AWS are not affected by the response output.

Three tasks from the D-KEFS Design Fluency subtest, adapted for digital administration, were also presented to the participants. Using a computer mouse, the participants drew designs within a booklet-style interface featuring rows of boxes, each containing an array of dots. The first task, Filled Dots, required the participants to connect dots using exactly four non-isolated straight lines to create as many unique designs as possible within 60 seconds. The second Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering task, Empty Dots Only, followed the identical rules, but the boxes contained both empty and filled dots, and the participants were instructed to use only the empty dots to form the designs.

Performance on these two tasks was later combined for scoring. The final task, Switching, was based on the same principles, but the participants were asked to alternate between using filled and empty dots within each design. Following the completion of all tasks, the participants received a written debriefing.

Analysis Group differences in Neuropsychological profiles

To understand group differences (specifically between self-reported AWS and self-reported dyslexic adults), we performed an independent-samples t-test for each standardized test (see Table 2). All statistical analyses were conducted using R (version 4.5.2), with a significance level (alpha) set at 0.05 (two-tailed). Key packages used were 'tidyverse' for data handling and 'effsize' for calculating effect sizes (Cohen's d). To control for Type I error in unplanned comparisons, a Holm-Bonferroni correction was applied to the reported p-values.

All data visualizations were generated using ggidst (version 3.3.3) packages in R.

To transparently illustrate both individual-level variability and group-level distribution, we created multi-panel raincloud plots for each cognitive and language measure, plus significant effects for phonemic and semantic fluency measures. Each plot integrates: (1) jittered raw data points reflecting individual participant scores, (2) centralized boxplots displaying group medians and interquartile ranges, and (3) probability density slabs to show the overall shape and density of distribution for each group (NT, PWD, and PWS).

Composite scores and correlations

Given the participant pool size (N = 186), it was necessary to reduce the initial 15 variables to just eight to ensure the reliability of the subsequent Principal Component Analysis (PCA), as it typically requires five to 10 participants per variable (Kass & Tinsley, 1979). This reduction in the number of variables was also performed to eliminate redundancy caused by highly correlated measures (e.g., Andrews & Lo, 2012; Elsherif et al., 2021 Elsherif et al., , 2022 Elsherif et al., , 2025;; Holmes et al., 2014).

To prepare the data, scores from the individual cognitive measures were z-transformed using the scale() function in R to make the measures more comparable. Next, Pearson's productmoment correlations (r) were calculated to confirm that the measures intended for grouping were sufficiently intercorrelated. This confirmation of strong intercorrelation (e.g., nonword repetition and memory for digits reflecting phonological short-term memory; Wagner et al., 1999) validated the assumption that these measures reflected a single underlying construct.

The z-scores were computed via scaling and the centre function in R. These related measures were then averaged to create composite scores. This averaging process reduced the overall number of variables entered into the PCA, while also ensuring that the combined score represented a unified construct rather than disparate measures.

Prior to conducting the Principal Component Analysis (PCA), we evaluated the suitability of the dataset for dimensionality reduction using the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) measure of sampling adequacy and Bartlett's test of sphericity. A KMO value exceeding .5 (Field, 2009) and a significant result in Bartlett's test (p < .001) were established as the criteria for determining appropriateness. Following these checks, PCA was performed in which the individual difference measures and the newly created composite scores were entered. This statistical technique identifies underlying factors (components) that explain most of the variability in the data. Both varimax (orthogonal) and oblique rotations were used to facilitate the interpretation of factor loadings, using the GPArotation package (Bernaards & Jennrich, 2005) in R (R Core Team, 2017). Decisions regarding the final component structure were based on the correlation between components; an oblique rotation was retained when component correlations exceeded .32, indicating meaningful overlap and justifying the use of correlated factors (Tabachnick et al., 2007).

Analysis of the verbal fluency tasks

There are varying approaches to quantifying cluster size. Traditionally, size is calculated by counting from the second word in a sequence (the n-1 method; see Mayr, 2002), which results in single words having a cluster size of zero. However, following the recommendations of Henry et al. (2015) and Smith-Spark et al. (2017) to avoid skewed data, we adopted the n method. Under this approach, every word is accounted for: single words that do not share a semantic or phonemic link with preceding or subsequent items are treated as distinct clusters of size one, while multi-word groupings are scored based on the total number of words they contain. Finally, the Switching trials (for both Semantic and Phonemic Fluency) were assessed based on the total number of correct responses and the total number of correct switching responses. Due to the nature of the responses made on the Design Fluency task, analyses were restricted to three measures, total responses, valid responses, and errors. In addition, category switching and design switching were assessed based on the total number of correct switching responses. The fine-grained analyses of phonemic and semantic fluency (i.e., switching and clustering) were conducted by the first author (MME), who has training in these metrics. Standardized scoring criteria were followed throughout. To ensure scoring consistency, a preliminary review of 10% of the dataset was independently evaluated by a research assistant, demonstrating high inter-rater reliability with 95% agreement. Following institutional ethics protocols, raw data were destroyed upon study completion, and only aggregated data were retained.

We selected hierarchical multiple regression as our primary analytical framework to assess the incremental predictive power of group membership (dyslexia and stuttering) after controlling for non-verbal IQ and broader cognitive domains. Separate hierarchical multiple regression analyses were used to determine whether the presence of dyslexia or stuttering For the hierarchical regression, a group factor (sum coded with AWS as the intercept) was entered as a fixed effect. The two components identified from the PCA analysis (lexical and phonological precision) were included as additional fixed effects in the model and were analyzed as continuous variables to maximize statistical power and precision. In instances where interaction terms reached statistical significance, we performed post-hoc visualization to interpret the directionality and nature of the effects. For these visualizations only, we categorized continuous PCA data into 'high' and 'low' groups via a median split, ensuring balanced group sizes. This approach was used solely as a diagnostic aid to plot interaction patterns and clarify the relationship between cognitive domains and fluency performance across groups. It did not inform the primary hypothesis testing, which relied entirely on continuous data. All comparisons were performed using two-tailed tests with the conventional p <.05 threshold for statistical significance (ɑ). The experiment was pre-registered at https://osf.io/yrdex, and all data, scripts, and materials are publicly available online at https://osf.io/wsa3b.

To maintain narrative clarity and focus on core theoretical questions, the text below highlights primary high-level findings and statistically significant predictors (p < .05) from the hierarchical regression models. Full step-by-step regression outputs, including non-significant model steps, full coefficient tables, and secondary measures, are comprehensively reported in Tables 6-7 and the accompanying OSF supplementary repository. regression aggregated at the participant level. This approach maintains the integrity of the group-level comparisons (neurodivergent profiles vs. neurotypical) while acknowledging the loss of trial -by-trial variance.

Results

Demographic variables

To validate group allocation, we conducted pairwise independent-samples t-tests across all groups for both the ARHQ and the stuttering questionnaire. This approach was chosen to assess specific key differences between groups, particularly as t-tests can be more robust to variances in group distributions (Field, 2009). Critically, while Figure 1 shows some overlap in self-reported stuttering traits (see Figure 1 for a distribution of self-reported stuttering traits across groups), self-reported AWS and self-reported dyslexic groups remained statistically distinct. The self-reported dyslexic adults scored higher than neurotypical adults in the stuttering questionnaire (t(122) = -2.43, p = .02, d = -0.44). The self-reported AWS group (M = 5.00, SD = 1.81) scored significantly higher on stuttering questionnaire than the neurotypical adult (M = 2.41, SD = 1.63) (t(122) = -8.31, p < .001, d = -1.50) and self-reported dyslexic adults (M = 3.16, SD = 1.77) (t(122) = 8.07, p < .001, d = -1.03).

In addition, we observed a clear double dissociation in scoring profiles, the self-reported dyslexic adults (M = 20.3, SD = 2.60) scored higher on the ARHQ than both the self-reported AWS (M = 14.6, SD = 4.85) (t(96.37) = 8.08, p < .001, Δ = 1.16) and the neurotypical groups (M = 3.77, SD = 2.26) (t(122) = -37.71, p = .001, d = -6.60). Neurotypical adults score lower than AWS in ARHQ (t(86.40) = -16.00, p < .001, Δ = -2.21). Although scores on the ARHQ-Brief and the stuttering questionnaire were moderately correlated (r = .49, p < .001), these significant group-level differences confirm that the neurodivergent populations remain statistically distinct in their respective primary diagnostic measures. Supplementary Table S1 provides the descriptive statistics (means, standard deviations, and ranges) for both the ARHQ-Brief and the Brief Stuttering Questionnaire across all three participant cohorts, alongside the explicit breakdown of participant counts relative to our screening thresholds to illustrate real-world comorbidity and score distribution.

In line with Elsherif et al. (2021), our findings also demonstrated that among 62 self-reported dyslexic adults, 22 stuttered as a child (a rate of childhood stuttering of 35%, which was significantly higher than 0% who stuttered as a childhood among the 62 neurotypical adults (G(1, N =124) = 34.05, p < .001, V = .52). Distribution of varying levels of stuttering by group. NT = neurotypical, SR-AWS = self-reported adults who stutter, and SR-DA = self-reported dyslexic adults. Each point represents a single participant, while the density plot above depicts the shape of the distribution. The overlaid boxplots present summary statistics of the median (central, dark vertical line), first and third quartile (the left-and right-most ends of the boxes) and the range of the values, bounded to within a distance of 1.5 times the inter-quartile range from the boxes (the whiskers).

Cognitive and language tests

Planned contrasts showed that the self-reported dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS performed worse than the neurotypical adults on CTOPP phoneme elision, memory for digits, nonword repetition, rapid letter naming phoneme reversal, TOWRE word and nonword reading (see Tables 2 and 3). However, the self-reported dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS did not differ in performance on the following measures: tests of TOWRE word and nonword reading, phoneme elision, memory for digits, nonword repetition, rapid letter naming (RLN), TIWRE and phoneme reversal. The self-reported dyslexic adults performed worse than the neurotypical adults and AWS on the spelling measure, while the neurotypical adults scored Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering higher on the TIWRE than the self-reported dyslexic adults. However, there was no significant difference between the self-reported dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS on the TIWRE.

There was no difference between the three groups in scores on Raven's Standard Progressive Matrices, Gray Silent Reading Test (GSRT), TIWRE, or British Picture Vocabulary Scale (BPVS-2). The self-reported dyslexic adults did not differ from the self-reported AWS and the NT in print exposure. To address task impurity issues and collinearity among individual neuropsychological measures, theoretically driven composite scores (Z-scored) were created for memory, reading fluency, and print exposure prior to dimensionality reduction (see Supplementary Materials for the full correlation matrix and composite construction details).

A Principal Component Analysis (PCA) with oblimin rotation was then conducted on these composites and the remaining individual measures to extract underlying cognitive constructs (see Supplementary Materials for full PCA parameters, factor loadings, and KMO/Bartlett's statistics). Two primary components emerged, accounting for 53% of the variance: Lexical Precision (comprising receptive vocabulary, print exposure, irregular word reading, and comprehension) and Phonological Precision (comprising reading fluency, phonological awareness, phoneme reversal, short-term memory, and spelling).

Independent-samples t-tests on the component scores revealed no significant group differences between any of the paired comparisons: self-reported AWS versus self-reported neurotypical adults (t(122) = -0.01, p = .992), self-reported AWS versus self-reported dyslexic adults (t(122) = -1.32, p = .19), and self-reported dyslexic adults versus neurotypical adults (t(122) = 1.36, p = .18).

For Phonological Precision, the neurotypical adults scored significantly higher than both the self-reported AWS (t(122) = 6.81, p < .001) and the self-reported dyslexic adults (t(122) = 7.38, p < .001), with no significant difference being found between the self-reported AWS and the self-reported dyslexic adults (t(122) = -0.35, p = .73). For an exploratory categorical subtyping analysis based on neurotypical-referenced PCA thresholds, see Supplementary Table S1b.

To confirm that these cognitive dimensions reflect stable latent constructs rather than methodspecific artifacts of Principal Component Analysis, we conducted a complementary Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA); these analyses yielded exceptional cross-method convergence (all r > .84; see Supplementary Note S1c).

Hierarchical regression analyses

Table 4 summarizes the descriptive statistics for all dependent variables across phonemic fluency, semantic fluency, category switching, and design fluency. Separate hierarchical multiple regression analyses were subsequently conducted to examine the distinct contributions of non-verbal intelligence (Step 1), phonological and lexical precision (Step 2), and diagnostic group status (Step 3). The figures illustrating these significant effects across fluency tasks are provided in Figures 2 and 3.

Total Word Production and Valid Responses Across Fluency Domains

For phonemic fluency (

Table 5), non-verbal IQ significantly predicted total responses and valid responses (Step 1), as did phonological and lexical precision (Step 2). In Step 3, both the presence of dyslexia and the presence of stuttering significantly predicted total responses and valid responses relative to neurotypical adults where the self-reported dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS produced fewer responses than neurotypical adults (Figure 2). For semantic fluency, total responses and correct responses were predicted by non-verbal IQ (Step 1), but were not significantly predicted by lexical or phonological precision or clinical group status in Steps 2 and 3. For design fluency (Table 6), total and valid responses were predicted by non-verbal IQ at Step 1, but neither lexical precision nor clinical status predicted output volume in Steps 2 or 3.

Cluster Size Across Phonemic and Semantic Fluency

For phonemic fluency, the presence of stuttering significantly predicted words per cluster, whereas dyslexia did not relative to neurotypical controls. Furthermore, when evaluating two neurodivergent groups (Step 3), the presence of dyslexia specifically predicted more words generated per cluster alongside a lower number of switches compared to the self-reported AWS group (Table 5) (Figure 2). For semantic fluency, words per cluster were predicted by

Raven's Standard Progressive Matrices at Step 1, but clinical group status (Step 3) did not predict cluster size outcomes.

Switching between Phonemic fluency and Semantic fluency

Hierarchical regressions on semantic and phonemic switching measures (Table 5) revealed of switches compared to neurotypical adults for both tasks (Figures 2 and 3). However, in phonemic fluency only, dyslexia predicted a lower relative switching rate compared to stuttering (Figure 2).

Discussion

The primary objective of the current study was to differentiate between the theoretical accounts of the phonological challenges experienced in dyslexia and stuttering. We adopted a staged analytical approach in which we first established broad cognitive indices via PCA before then seeing how these indices related to executive fluency performance.

The PCA distilled individual differences into lexical precision (vocabulary and print exposure) and phonological precision (short-term memory, phonological awareness, and retrieval; Elsherif et al., 2025).

Our individual differences results aligned with previous findings (e.g., Elsherif et al., 2021; see also Coalson & Byrd, 2015; Pelczarski & Yaruss, 2014; Spencer & Weber-Fox, 2014 for stuttering; Snowling, 2019; Smith-Spark et al., 2017, for dyslexia), suggesting that the self-reported AWS and the self-reported dyslexic adults show overlapping phonological processing profiles. Furthermore, both self-reported neurodivergent groups performed below the level of neurotypical adults on the TOWRE, a pattern consistent with shared challenge in rapid phonological decoding. This extensive overlap in self-reported cooccurring neurodivergent traits, including the proportion of self-reported AWS scoring within the mild dyslexia range and the notable reported history of childhood stuttering within the dyslexia group, highlights a shared challenge rooted in phonological processing that appears to cross clinical boundaries. However, these shared features coexist with clear behavioral dissociations. The self-reported dyslexic group showed uniquely poor spelling performance on the British Ability Scales (Elliott et al., 1996). Furthermore, on the TIWRE (Reynolds et al., 2007), self-reported dyslexic adults performed significantly below neurotypical controls, whereas the performance of AWS fell between these two groups.

Having established the underlying cognitive factors (lexical precision and phonological precision) via the PCA, we then compared the verbal fluency measures against non-verbal design fluency indices to provide a novel, granular dissociation between the two neurodivergent profiles.

Phonemic fluency was assessed using a composite score derived from performance in response to three initial phonemes ('F', 'A', and 'S'). For all three participant groups, the volume of both total and valid responses generated was primarily influenced by phonological precision, lexical precision, and dyslexia, stuttering, or both neurodivergent profiles. Our finding that the self-reported AWS and the self-reported dyslexic adults generated fewer correct items than the neurotypical controls aligns with an extensive body of literature that consistently documents phonemic fluency difficulties in English (e.g., Brosnan et al., 2002; Smith-Spark et al., 2017; Snowling et al., 1997) and other orthographies, including French (Plaza et al., 2002), Greek (Mengisidiou & Marshall, 2019), German (Landerl et al., 2009), Italian (Varvara et al., 2014), and Portuguese (Moura et al., 2015). Similarly, reduced phonemic fluency output has been observed in both children (Bahrami et al., 2014) and adults (Gkalitsiou & Werle, 2026; Lescht et al., 2022) who stutter.

On the phonemic fluency task, the self-reported dyslexic adults also produced significantly fewer switches than the neurotypical adults and the self-reported AWS, but the self-reported dyslexic and neurotypical adults did not differ in average cluster size. The lack of a relationship between self-reported dyslexia and cluster size in the current study is consistent with the previous literature, using verified diagnoses (Mielnik et al., 2015; Smith-Spark et al., 2017).

Conversely, the self-reported AWS scored lower in average cluster size than the neurotypical adults and the self-reported dyslexic adults, with the self-reported AWS showing fewer switches than neurotypical adults but significantly more than the self-reported dyslexic group.

While evidence at this fine-grained measurement level remains sparse in the literature on executive fluency in stuttering, our findings are not consistent with previous research where no group difference in clustering was reported (Gkalitsiou & Werle, 2026). One explanation for this discrepancy relates to differing choices of response modality. The present study used a typing-based format (which was similar in approach to that of Lescht et al., 2022) instead of naming responses.

Critically, our use of a typing response modality may have disrupted the internal monitoring systems and auditory feedback loops that AWS use to stabilise speech production (Civier et Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering al., 2010; Postma & Kolk, 1993). As a result, we may have unmasked a degraded phonological representation that is typically compensated for during speech. This would explain the reduced cluster sizes observed in the current study. Without the reinforcement of the auditory loop, the ability to 'group' sounds together effectively is compromised. In contrast, the self-reported AWS showed lower scores in switching than the neurotypical adults, but not the self-reported dyslexic adults. For the self-reported AWS, switching may represent a lifelong executive control habit. Adults who stutter frequently use circumlocution, wherein they switch between target words to avoid feared sounds and maintain perceived fluency (Tichenor & Yaruss, 2019). The lower switching scores observed in our study likely reflect a fundamental, persistent, and modality-free executive search constraint. While average clustering measures require careful interpretation due to their sensitive nature (Henry et al., 2015; Smith-Spark et al., 2017), the current study's robust sample size provides the necessary power to identify these subtle differences in phonological persistence manifested by the the two neurodivergent groups.

The reduced total word production in both the self-reported dyslexic adults and the selfreported AWS suggests that when a task makes explicit demands on phonological processing, sound-based representations are less accessible than they are in neurotypical adults.

However, a critical divergence between self-reported dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS appears in the implicit access measured by clustering. The self-reported dyslexic adults produced cluster sizes comparable to neurotypical controls. This finding suggests that their underlying phonological representations remain intact and are accessible through automatic, implicit pathways when the conscious processing load is minimized. For the self-reported dyslexic group, this pattern aligns directly with the access to the phonological representation hypothesis (Boets et al., 2013). The phonemic fluency difficulty appears to be localized to the explicit retrieval mechanisms that interface with lexical selection. In contrast, the self-reported AWS profile reflects a more pervasive difficulty in phonological processing. Because the selfreported AWS group demonstrated significantly reduced clustering in phonemic fluency compared with both neurotypical and self-reported dyslexic peers, the findings indicate a Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering breakdown in both explicit and implicit access to their phonological representations. For the self-reported AWS, the difficulty may not be merely one of effortful retrieval but may result from a fundamental instability in the automatic activation of phonologically related neighbors within the onset of the words. Consequently, while the self-reported dyslexic adults' verbal fluency difficulty may be characterized as a 'retrieval bottleneck,' the self-reported AWSrelated phonemic fluency profile aligns more closely with the phonological representation hypothesis (Ziegler & Goswami, 2005), arising from a global challenge in phonological momentum, with this affecting the system's ability to maintain automatic activation within the phonological network during task performance.

Our findings as they relate to stuttering, therefore, raise several issues relating to the theoretical distinction between an imprecise phonological representation (an encoding/planning challenge) and an intact phonological representation that is difficult to translate, select, or initiate into a motor program. Within neurocomputational frameworks of speech production, such as GODIVA (Bohland et al., 2010) and DIVA (Guenther et al., 2006), these two processes are anatomically and functionally distinct. According to these models, phonological frame selection and sequence planning reside in the left posterior inferior frontal sulcus (pIFS), whereas motor programs are represented in the Speech Sound Map within the ventral premotor cortex (vPMC) and are gated for initiation via basal ganglia-thalamocortical loops (preSMA/SMA (e.g., Bohland et al., 2010; Guenther et al., 2006). Standard models of stuttering (e.g., Bohland et al., 2010; Guenther et al., 2006) typically attribute dysfluencies to intact phonological representations paired with impaired timing, faulty feedforward control, or elevated basal ganglia initiation thresholds. In contrast, our data point toward lower phonological precision upstream in pIFS. When phonological codes in pIFS lack distinct sharpness, the feedforward activation signals targeting vPMC motor programs are likely to become ambiguous. Consequently, the basal ganglia premotor loops responsible for gating motor initiation must operate under heightened uncertainty, thereby increasing competition among candidate motor programs and delaying initiation thresholds. Crucially, an imprecise phonological representation does not preclude motor-level initiation deficits; rather, it introduces upstream noise into downstream translation. This proposed mechanism directly bridges linguistic planning and motor execution, aligning with the EXPLAN model of stuttering (Howell & Au-Yeung, 2002), in which dysfluencies arise when delayed linguistic planning (PLAN) fails to deliver a timely, fully specified structure to motor execution (EX).

The current study used a typing paradigm. As a consequence of this design decision, questions may arise as to how responding in this modality applies to the language-related challenges experienced by AWS. The typed response modality may reflect covert articulation (i.e., a subvocal motor planning constraint; van Thorre & Vannuscorps, 2026). During written language production, adults who do not stutter engage in internal speech-motor simulation and silent subvocal rehearsal to support phonological working memory and sequence verification (Baddeley, 2003; Postma & Kolk, 1993). According to such internal feedforward models, the internal feedforward models simulate motor commands prior to overt execution.

If AWS experience difficulties translating phonological representations into timed motor plans, then this constraint may also be found during covert, internal speech simulation.

Consequently, typing bypasses overt vocalisation by reducing blocks, but it does not remove the covert motor assembly component. If subvocal planning follows a different computational routine, the automatic activation and clustering of phonologically related items may reflect unique network dynamics. However, we acknowledge that our typing paradigm clearly cannot capture covert vocal tract kinematics. Future research would require a co-registration of highdensity electroencephalography and electromyography to determine whether covert speechmotor simulation explains latency peaks during typed production.

Semantic fluency was assessed using a single category, while category switching was evaluated via a dual-category task. Consistent with previous research on both dyslexia (Mengsidiou et al., 2020; Smith-Spark et al., 2017) and stuttering (Bahrami et al., 2014; Gkalitsiou & Werle, 2026), no significant group differences were found in total semantic output or between-category switching. However, a significant participant group-related divergence emerged in the internal dynamics associated with performance. While all the participant groups engaged in both within-category and between-category shifts, both the self-reported Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering dyslexic adults and the self-reported AWS demonstrated a disproportionate reduction in within-category switches but similar between-category switches relative to their neurotypical peers. This finding aligns with patterns previously reported in dyslexia (Mengsidiou et al., 2020), but also it extends the current understanding of AWS by statistically demonstrating in self-reported AWS a trend previously noted in the literature (Gkalitsiou & Werle, 2026).

Crucially, this pattern indicates that the linguistic difficulties experienced by these populations is not characterized by a general executive failure to shift mental sets, but rather by a specific selection and competition difficulty occurring within closely related linguistic representations.

For design fluency, the three participant groups generated similar numbers of unique designs with similar numbers of errors. Similarly, category-switching performance did not differ across the neurotypical adults, the self-reported adults with dyslexia, and the self-reported AWS (e.g., Bertola et al., 2014; Troyer, 2000). These findings indicate that the executive fluency difficulties in dyslexia and stuttering are specific to phonologically-and semantically-mediated processing instead of a domain-general challenge in processing speed or executive control.

Although executive fluency paradigms carry inherent task-impurity problems (e.g., Anderle & Venuti, 2026), the use of an equivalent non-verbal baseline measure demonstrates that the executive "machinery" for initiation, flexibility, and switching remains intact in both neurodivergent groups.

While the current study, therefore, provides important and novel insights into the cognitivelinguistic profiles of the self-reported AWS and the self-reported dyslexic adults, several design limitations must be acknowledged.

Our modification of the verbal fluency tasks from an oral to a typed response format introduced a distinct methodological trade-off. On the one hand, typing effectively isolates cognitivelinguistic retrieval by bypassing oral-motor execution constraints, physical speech blocks, and social-evaluative anxiety, while simultaneously facilitating large-scale web-based administration. On the other hand, this same modification introduces task-demand asymmetries across the two neurodivergent profiles. For the self-reported AWS, typed administration decouples phonological retrieval from speech-motor planning and execution, a core interface in spoken disfluency frameworks (e.g., DIVA/GODIVA models; Bohland et al., 2010; Guenther et al., 2006). Conversely, for the self-reported dyslexic adults, typing imposes additional orthographic assembly and fine-motor planning demands (Košir et al., 2024; Sumner et al., 2012). Because cognitive resources have to be shared between spelling verification and lexical search (Sumner et al., 2012), inter-response latencies can become inflated. Consequently, it is crucial to dissociate general processing or typing execution speed from underlying linguistic retrieval.

Despite these manual sequencing demands, several lines of evidence indicate that they do not systematically bias our results. First, because manual requirements are balanced across participant groups, any generalized motor difficulties in AWS would have suppressed performance uniformly across all the tasks. Instead, group differences were task-specific: they were evident in phonemic fluency, but absent in semantic fluency, switching responses, and design fluency. This pattern confirms that the observed effects reflect specific cognitive phonological constraints rather than general motor or output limitations. However, it should be noted that evidence regarding general manual sequencing deficits in AWS remains equivocal (Cross & Luper, 1983; Olander et al., 2010; Zelaznik et al., 1997). Second, concerns regarding an orthographic bottle in dyslexia are affected by procedural controls and qualitative errors are mitigated against by the instructions given to the participants and the nature of their error responses. The participants received explicit instructions to ignore typographical errors, scoring accommodated phonologically plausible spellings, and produced errors that were predominantly morphological substitutions and repetitions (e.g., "fly, flying, flew") instead of the surface-level orthographic breakdowns typical of dyslexia (Afonso et al., 2022; Connelly et al., 2006; Elsherif et al., 2025). This points to a deeper lexical selection challenge that persists even when oral output channels are bypassed. While individual typing proficiency and hardware latency were not explicitly measured, our moderate sample size (N = 62 per group) effectively distributed this variance across conditions. Nevertheless, future research using a similar paradigm should incorporate a standardized baseline typing assessment as a covariate.

More broadly, our adoption of a typed paradigm highlights the trade-off between specialized, in person laboratory studies and well-powered remote paradigms. By leveraging remote administration via Prolific and online communication, our study recruited a moderate-powered sample (N = 62 per group) that significantly exceeded those reported in previous work (e.g., Gkalitsiou et al., 2018, N = 32 AWS; Smith-Spark et al., 2017, N = 28 dyslexia). This larger sample size directly addresses recent replicability concerns raised by Leung et al. (2026) and van Bergen et al. (2026) regarding the reliability of the findings obtained in the study of dyslexia and related language neurodivergences. Coupled with strict group matching on age, gender, print exposure, and IQ, the current sample provides a stable foundation for our findings, even though the exclusion of spoken output naturally limits direct generalizability to real-world oral communication. Future work combining synchronized eye-tracking or keystroke logging could focus on orthographic planning latencies that underlies lexical-phonological search mechanics.

There are also limitations which relate to participant recruitment and diagnostic classification that must also be acknowledged. The dyslexia cohort was recruited based on self-reported formal diagnoses without the participants having to state the nature or extent of previous phonics-based interventions, which can influence task performance in phonological processing tasks (e.g., elision; Elbro & Petersen, 2004). While remote testing precludes inperson clinical validation, group allocation was controlled using validated screening instruments, which provide reliable, highly specific classifications of lifelong neurodivergent traits (Feng et al., 2021; Garvin & Krishnan, 2021; Pennington & LeFly, 2001) in the context of the financial and systemic barriers restricting adult clinical re-evaluation (Ahuvia et al., 2026). Although our primary analyses treated groups categorically, our behavioural data (see the supplementary materials) revealed an overlap: 96.8% of self-reported AWS scored in the mild dyslexia range on the standardized screeners, alongside a high prevalence of selfreported childhood stuttering among the self-reported adult dyslexics. Instead of undermining the group classifications, this surface-level overlap emphasises the need for transdiagnostic frameworks. Moving beyond binary clinical labels, dimensional and latent-variable approaches Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering (e.g., structural equation modelling) would seem to be essential to mapping out how interacting cognitive dimensions predict executive fluency outcomes across different neurodivergent profiles.

Finally, potential domain-general confounds were carefully controlled through our task battery design. To avoid the lexical access confounds inherent to linguistically based executive tasks like the Stroop task (Elsherif et al., 2025; Stroop, 1935), we used a non-verbal design fluency task to isolate domain-general executive function and found that there were no neurodivergence-related effects. Furthermore potential concerns regarding earlier cognitive fatigue in neurodivergent populations (Nicolson & Fawcett, 1990; Starkweather & Gottwald, 1990) are mitigated by our fixed-task order. Core cognitive-linguistic measures are administered early in the testing session, whereas tasks showing comparable performance across groups were positioned at the conclusion where fatigue would most likely exacerbate group differences.

To conclude, our results indicate that, in verbal fluency performance at the very least, dyslexia reflects a challenge to access phonological representations, while stuttering is defined by the phonological representation itself. These findings advance our understanding of speech-and language-related neurodivergence by demonstrating that, while overlapping phonological processing differences frame a shared cognitive continuum, distinct behavioural outcomes preserve critical diagnostic boundaries. This explanatory framework reconciles how these two groups share surface-level phonological challenges while operating under different cognitive mechanisms. Ultimately, we argue that these findings carry profound theoretical and translational weight for how the architecture of neurodivergent cognitive profiles is conceptualized. At a theoretical level, our results challenge rigid paradigms by demonstrating that developmental stuttering and dyslexia are best understood through a shared, spectrumbased lens characterized by overlapping difficulties in the phonological system. By isolating these challenges to phonological representation and access, while also ruling out a global executive difficulty, we provide a more precise cognitive map of how linguistic precision constrains fluency across distinct neurodivergent populations. From an applied perspective, Running head: Verbal fluency dyslexia and stuttering we feel that these insights should shift clinical and educational paradigms in relation to neurodivergence. Rather than treating reading challenges and speech difficulties in absolute isolation, we argue that clinicians, educators, and speech-language therapists should recognize their shared phonological roots. Interventions targeted at strengthening phonological precision and managing lexical competition could yield downstream benefits for both literacy acquisition and speech fluency, offering a unified, cross-diagnostic framework that better serves the complex needs of neurodivergent adults.
